Abstract: This article presents a conformal transmit-reflect array (CTRA) that enables bidirectional multibeam switching through a single metasurface aperture for emergency communication applications. To realize reconfigurable bidirectional beam switching, a transmit-reflect integrated element (TRIE) is developed by combining a polarization-selective transmissive structure with a complementary-symmetric reflective structure. The proposed element enables independent control of the cross-polarized transmission and reflection phases while exhibiting stable performance under oblique incidence. To support practical conformal implementations, a geometrical-optics-based phase compensation method is applied to cylindrical geometries, enabling effective wavefront control across the conformal aperture. By incorporating a polarization-switchable feed array (PSFA), multifunctional beam switching is achieved in the forward, backward, and bidirectional operating modes. The PSFA is configured as a nonuniform curved linear array near the focal region, enabling feed-selective beam switching within $\pm 30^\circ$ in both the $+z$ and $-z$ directions through selective excitation of the corresponding feed elements. The full-wave simulation results agree well with the measurements. The proposed CTRA achieves peak gains of 24.1 and 24.8 dBi in the forward and backward modes, respectively. The maximum aperture efficiencies in the forward and backward operating states are 28.4$\%$ and 33.4$\%$. These results demonstrate the effectiveness and flexibility of the proposed CTRA, making it a promising candidate for adaptive emergency communication systems.

Abstract: Modular multilevel converter (MMC) has become an attractive technology for high-power electronics applications thanks to its high energy efficiency, high power rating, high modularity, flexible scalability, and superior output waveform quality. It has attracted considerable research attention, and numerous submodule (SM) variants have been developed for different objectives. Among them, the half-bridge SM (HBSM) is the most widely adopted thanks to its simplicity, but it cannot interrupt DC-side short-circuit currents. Various SM topologies with fault-blocking capability have been proposed. These SM topologies contain additional switches and passive components compared to the HBSM, leading to higher capital and operating costs. Aiming at these issue, an SM topology capable to block DC-side fault-currents with a reduced component count is proposed in this paper. The proposed SM includes two variants, one employs only two fully-controlled switches. An operation and control scheme for the proposed SM are also developed and presented in the paper. The functionality of the proposed SM is validated by experiment, and the value of the SM is demonstrated through a test simulation for an high-voltage direct-current (HVDC) transmission system.

</details>

Abstract: Additive manufacturing is transforming the creation of materials and parts, enabling the design and manufacturing of multifunctional parts with tailored properties. One compelling example is the combination of a porous core and a non-porous surface, which offers significant advantages in terms of weight reduction, material efficiency, strength and wear resistance. Directed Energy Deposition (DED) processes enable the transition from porous core to non-porous part surface by closing the pores. However, the surface-layer of porous substrates presents a substantial challenge to achieving consistent track heights during DED, directly impacting the final part geometry and performance. Therefore, precise control of the deposition process is crucial for realising the full potential of the multifunctional parts. In order to address this challenge, this work presents a conceptual approach for track height control during Laser-based Directed Energy Deposition (DED-LB) on porous substrates. First, the DED-LB process and the associated measurement setup are explained, along with a discussion of their limitations. The focus then shifts to the control problem, beginning with system identification to characterise the process dynamics. Subsequently, a selection of control strategies is investigated, as well as their compatibility with the process. This research provides a foundational step toward reliable additive manufacturing on porous substrates, and thus toward producing multifunctional parts.

</details>

Abstract: Model-based design (MBD) is a mature framework for designing the current controllers of modular multilevel converters (MMCs) in high-voltage direct current (HVDC) transmission systems. In MBD, linear state-space models are preferred for their good trade-off between accurate dynamics prediction and simplicity. However, first-principle derivations often neglect relevant MMC dynamics, which can hinder controller performance. Data-driven (DD) methods can deliver less biased models that capture parameter uncertainties, control-loop interactions, and grid dynamics, yet they remain under-exploited for controlling MMC currents. This paper studies DD current control method based on integral Subspace Predictive Control (iSPC), synthesized directly from input--output data measured in a high fidelity real-time digital simulator (RTDS), and proposes an initial guideline outlining the main steps for its systematic application to MMC current control. The proposed iSPC is compared against state-of-the-art DD solution, and the findings are validated through simulation studies in MATLAB/Simulink.

</details>

Abstract: Autonomous systems operating in dynamic environments often involve multiple interacting agents, giving rise to high-dimensional safety evaluation problems. Hamilton--Jacobi (HJ) reachability provides rigorous finite-horizon safety guarantees, but solving a global HJ partial differential equation (PDE) over the entire state space leads to prohibitive computational complexity. In this paper, we introduce Geometry-Evolving Barrier Functions (GEBFs), a novel representation for dynamic safety evaluation that propagates local safety geometry of the solution to a terminal-value HJ PDE. By evolving the value, gradient, and curvature information along a Hamiltonian-induced reference trajectory, GEBFs provide a compact representation of the local HJ safety landscape. Under suitable local regularity assumptions, we establish consistency between the GEBF and the value, gradient, and Hessian of the HJ solution along an HJ-optimal characteristic. Building on these theoretical results, we develop an iterative algorithm that couples local geometric propagation with trajectory refinement. We further establish a trajectory-wise one-sided relationship between the GEBF and HJ value function, together with a fixed-point consistency result for the iterative scheme. Experiments on widely adopted agent--obstacle collision-avoidance benchmarks, across low- and high-dimensional systems, demonstrate that GEBFs accurately capture the evolution of dynamic safety boundaries, while achieving substantially lower computational cost than conventional HJ reachability.

</details>

Abstract: Conventional power-swing blocking (PSB) schemes for distance protection face new and emerging challenges as the penetration levels of inverter-based resources (IBRs) increase. Several papers highlight interoperability issues concerning faster power swings and unconventional impedance trajectories. Revised relay settings are proposed in attempts to deal with the problems. However, the blinder-based PSB methodology suffers from being dependent on the speed of the apparent impedance trajectory over the tiny region between the blinders, making it difficult to distinguish between fast swings and faults. Moreover, blinders have the inherent limitation that they cannot operate if the measured impedance is inside the blinders when the fault is cleared and immediately enters the distance zones during the first swing cycle - an issue often overlooked in the current literature. This paper proposes a novel PSB scheme based on higher-order incremental quantities (IQs) for difficult swing scenarios with IBRs and synchronous generators (SGs). PSCAD/EMTDC simulations of an SG and an implemented grid-forming (GFM) inverter validate its superior performance compared to conventional dual-blinder PSB schemes. The impacts of GFM inverters on the impedance measured by distance relays during the fault-to-swing transition and first power swing cycle are also discussed.

</details>

Abstract: This paper extends the Dubins Airplane Method to generate trajectories for fixed-wing aircraft that remain within flight performance limits in the presence of uniform wind. Within the field of runtime-efficient trajectory generation for fixed-wing aircraft, the Dubins Airplane Method represents a well-received and widely adopted compromise between algorithmic complexity, runtime efficiency, and solution quality. However, its simplistic kinematic model restricts its ability to generate safe and feasible trajectories, especially in the presence of wind. As a mathematical foundation to overcome these limitations, a set of closed-form equations are derived that relate flight performance constraints to kinematic constraints. Building upon existing methods that use trochoids for planning of 2D trajectories in uniform wind, this paper presents a set of iterative algorithms for planning versatile 3D trajectories composed of trochoidal or circular turns and straight flight segments, with constant vertical speed, constant thrust or smooth longitudinal transitions. The presented methods comprise a flexible and modular framework for runtime-efficient generation of performance-aware trajectories based on parametric curves without the need for numerical trajectory representations.

</details>

Abstract: Multisensory Physical AI systems operating in isolated conditions must make timely and reliable decisions within strict energy and hardware budgets. In this context, the present analytical study investigates the feasibility of reliable multimodal sensor fusion using a neuromorphic approach based on memristor-based Integrate-and-Fire neuron circuits driven by aggressively compressed representations of sensory information. Rather than relying on fixed processing and learning parameters, the proposed framework introduces scenario-dependent adaptability into individual stages of the neuromorphic decision-making pipeline, including sensor-data compression, nonlinear spike encoding, reliability assessment, and memristive learning-forgetting dynamics. By coupling multiple sensor inputs through adaptive memristive synapses, the framework investigates how reliable and correlated observations can strengthen learned associations, while weak or obsolete relationships gradually decay through nonlinear forgetting. The resulting neuronal responses are evaluated across ten representative driving scenarios derived from the NuScenes dataset by comparing their encoded output patterns with predefined synthetic ground-truth codes representing the expected actuator responses. The simulations demonstrate that scenario-adaptive activity-vector sizing achieves a 61.5% reduction in retained sensory information, while improving mean bit-wise agreement from approximately 55% to 61.2% compared with a fixed maximum-resolution configuration. These findings highlight the potential of introducing scenario-aware adaptability directly into the building blocks of a neuromorphic system, providing a foundation for future hardware implementations that could significantly transform the capabilities of Physical AI systems operating under stringent resource and environmental constraints.

</details>

Abstract: Excitable spikers are attractive systems for sparse, event-driven computation. A spiking primitive composed of a linear frequency selector closed in positive feedback around a high-gain saturating nonlinearity has recently been proposed as a minimal excitable system. This paper asks which bursting classes that primitive can produce with two elementary selectors: a first-order high-pass filter, and a second-order resonant band-pass filter. The nonlinearity decides whether the system fires, and where the rheobase sits. The selector decides whether rest and repetitive firing can coexist, and therefore which bursting class is available. We show that the high-pass never makes rest and firing coexist, whatever the nonlinearity, and that no linear slow feedback branch can then produce bursting. With a piecewise linear nonlinearity, it gives type I excitability, and a second copy of the primitive gives parabolic bursting. The resonant band-pass gives a Hopf, type II excitability, and a subcritical onset above a computable gain, hence a window where rest and firing coexist. A single additional slow linear feedback then gives elliptic bursting. We also show that the resting point is unique for both selectors, which makes square-wave bursting structurally unavailable. Closed-form thresholds, firing rates, and criticality conditions are given and validated numerically.

</details>

Abstract: Boolean networks (BNs) are widely used to model gene regulatory networks and other biological systems. In this paper, we investigate the minimal controllability problem for BNs subject to degree constraints. We first establish three new best-case upper bounds for XOR-BNs whose dependency graphs are $k$-regular. We then introduce classes of BNs that are invariant under cyclically partite transformation (CPT-invariant) and prove theorems connecting BN controllability with the surjectivity of vector-valued Boolean functions. Finally, by combining an analysis of this surjectivity with a probabilistic argument, we derive a new general lower bound on the minimum number of control nodes required for majority-type threshold BNs with $(2k+1)$-regular dependency graphs.

Abstract: Longitudinal velocity is a core input for vehicle control, driver-assistance, and automated driving functions. Production wheel-speed sensors deliver only a few pulses per revolution, so they lose resolution below 1 km/h, the regime in which automated parking and traffic-jam assistance operate. The motor speed from the electric machine's resolver stays finely resolved down to standstill but reaches the wheels only through a compliant drivetrain. We therefore estimate the longitudinal velocity with an unscented Kalman filter (UKF) on a double-track model adapted to low velocities and incorporate the motor speed along two routes, one purely physical and one hybrid. The first augments the model with an explicit torsional spring-damper drivetrain, making the motor speed a state of the estimator. The second feeds the output of a neural network-based virtual wheel-speed sensor into the UKF as a pseudo-measurement. Both routes are compared against two baselines, a wheel-speed-only configuration and one that adds the motor-speed sensor but treats the driveline as rigid. Because the model is numerically stiff near standstill, the comparison rests on a systematic study of explicit and semi-implicit discretization schemes. All four configurations are evaluated against an inertial reference in real-world test drives with two distinct series-production electric vehicles, anonymized as Vehicle A and Vehicle B for confidentiality. Below 1 km/h, both proposed estimators lower the velocity root-mean-square error by at least 54% and 45% against both baselines on Vehicle A. Carried over zero-shot to Vehicle B without re-tuning on target-vehicle data, they lower it by at least 41% and 51%. Both estimators therefore carry across vehicles without a new identification campaign.

</details>

Abstract: This work identifies a fundamental, controller-independent limitation in reusable launch vehicle powered descent: a sudden $180^\circ$ phase transition in the open-loop transfer functions from the control inputs to the lateral drift and drift rate. The transition occurs inside the flight envelope, reverses the effective control direction, and can destabilize the closed loop system under uncertainty. An analytical condition governing this transition is derived, its physical mechanism is established, and its dependence on vehicle mass, aerodynamic properties, and geometry is characterized. An Incremental Nonlinear Dynamic Inversion (INDI)-based control architecture is then developed to inherently address this limitation and is tuned via $\mathcal{H}_\infty$ Closed Loop Shaping to provide local robustness and performance guarantees. The internal dynamics introduced by the INDI inversion are analytically derived and proven to be stable throughout the flight envelope. The proposed controller is compared against a robust gain-scheduled baseline controller through linear stability analysis and an extensive nonlinear Monte Carlo campaign. Both controllers achieve 100% success across the considered Monte Carlo campaign, though the INDI-based controller provides improved tracking performance, superior nominal and dispersed stability margins, requires fewer controller design points, and reduces controller tuning complexity.

</details>


### [336] [Optical Network Digital Twin: From Digital Coherent Transmission to Intelligent Network Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08217)
*Hideki Nishizawa,Enrico Miotto,Andrea Rosso,Takahito Tanimura,Zehao Wang,Mingzhe Han,Hiroyuki Ishihara,Andrea D'Amico,Agastya Raj,Rocco D'Ingillo,Takeo Sasai,Giacomo Borraccini,Toru Mano,Renato Ambrosone,Behnam Shariati,Oscar Gonz'alez-de-Dios,Arturo Mayoral L'opez de Lerma,Jun-ichi Kani,Manabu Yoshimoto,Tingjun Chen,Marco Ruffini,Daniel Kilper,Vittorio Curri*

Abstract: Optical Network Digital Twins (ONDTs) are emerging as a key framework for representing, synchronizing, and utilizing the physical state of optical networks for quality-of-transmission (QoT) estimation, optimization, and intelligent operation. This survey provides a comprehensive view of ONDTs, spanning digital coherent transmission and physical-layer observability to intelligent network operations. We systematically organize ONDT technologies from ecosystem, functional, control, and implementation architecture perspectives and review the enabling technologies required to construct, synchronize, and utilize digital representations of physical optical networks. As a unifying framework to describe both ONDT design and validation, we introduce a categorization based on the following figures of merit: Fidelity, Dynamicity, and Deployability (FDD). In particular, we emphasize fit-for-purpose Fidelity, where modeling effort focuses on the physical effects of components rather than solely maximizing model accuracy, favoring scalability and generalizability. Furthermore, experimental and field validations are examined to evaluate the practical applicability of ONDT technologies. We further examine experimental and field validations, from physics-based QoT estimation and measurement-informed model refinement to field synchronization, service provisioning, multi-domain observability, and infrastructure characterization. Finally, we discuss the complementary roles of ONDTs and AI agents toward autonomous optical networks and identify key research directions for practical deployment.

</details>

Abstract: This paper develops a dither-free output-feedback extremum-seeking controller for pre-stabilized nonlinear dynamic systems with unknown steady-state performance maps. The controller uses only the measured performance, the applied parameter, and internal states. Within a local state--parameter domain, integral sliding mode enforces equality between the applied parameter rate and a prescribed nonzero search velocity after finite-time capture under bounded matched rate-channel perturbations. This motion lets a super-twisting differentiator and rate normalization recover a gradient-sign surrogate without demodulation or averaging. A ramp-corrected moving-equilibrium coordinate cancels the first-order ramp-induced plant term and yields explicit flow, reversal, recovery, and dynamic-remainder bounds. The resulting local state--parameter tube supplies the regularity needed for finite-time exact differentiation. Combined with a deadbanded hybrid relay, these results establish local practical convergence to an explicit neighborhood of the unknown optimum. An 80-state tubular-reactor benchmark and paired held-out Monte Carlo tests compare a causal sampled realization with perturbation ESC and a rate-informed super-twisting comparator. The proposed realization succeeds in all 200 held-out cases and attains the smallest upper-tail objective gap under the stated tuning protocol. A matched-rate sweep connects performance degradation to applied-rate mismatch, near-stall motion, reversal, and regularization attenuation.

</details>

Abstract: Building electrification projections are typically developed at aggregated geographic levels to support bulk power system analysis, even though a significant portion of the infrastructure investments required to accommodate electrification occurs within distribution systems. Existing methodologies to disaggregate load growth projections onto distribution networks are based on context-specific analyses that require detailed data on building electricity consumption, distribution feeders, and geographic parcels, which are often proprietary and unavailable for policy and economic impact studies. This paper presents a methodology to disaggregate county-level building electrification projections onto representative distribution feeders and evaluate their impact on distribution upgrade costs. The proposed approach combines bulk system end-use electrification projections with representative feeder sets to generate building-level electrification measures. These measures are then used to create load profiles that are consistent with county-level projections while preserving the spatial diversity of electrification demand required for distribution infrastructure analysis. Using a case study of Genesee County, in Michigan, the results show that the proposed methodology is able to capture a diversity of feeder-level responses to electrification and represent the different realities that building electrification produces within the distribution system. This allows to represent localized load variations and capture more realistic distribution system upgrade costs driven by electrification.

</details>

Abstract: The rapid growth of large-scale data centers (DCs) is increasing electricity demand, market volatility, and operating costs. Battery energy storage systems (BESSs) can mitigate these challenges by shifting electricity purchases from high-price to low-price periods. This paper proposes a real-time BESS dispatch framework using polynomial diffusion models (PDMs) to forecast both electricity prices and DC loads. Leveraging the closed-form conditional expectations of PDMs, the forecasts are integrated into a dynamic programming (DP) framework to minimize electricity procurement costs. Across the evaluated BESS configurations, the proposed approach achieves 2.63 times the cost savings of a rule-based (RB) strategy.

</details>

Result: 解析错误

Abstract: Remote robot surgery and tele-ultrasound increasingly rely on real-time streaming under strict bandwidth constraints, making lossy compression unavoidable. Since the receiving end of these clinical pipelines is increasingly a machine rather than a human observer, codec selection must be guided by downstream task accuracy rather than perceptual fidelity. We evaluate three widely deployed codecs, H.264/AVC, HEVC, and AV1, on prostate ultrasound segmentation, reporting task-aware compression performance with region overlap (Dice) and boundary accuracy (HD95) as the quality metrics. To compare codecs fairly, we match their operating points at a common perceptual-quality (SSIM) level. We establish, per codec, the lowest bitrate that preserves acceptable segmentation accuracy, reporting the 5th-percentile Dice alongside the mean to expose worst-case degradation. All three codecs preserve segmentation quality (mean Dice $\geq$ 0.84) down to approximately 0.07-0.20 bits per voxel (bpv). Below this point, AV1's accuracy declines gradually, while H.264's drops sharply. As a result, H.264 needs 82% more bitrate than AV1 to reach the same region-overlap accuracy (Dice), and 104.7% more to reach the same boundary accuracy (HD95).

</details>

Abstract: Background and Objective: Bridging kidney MRI with patient-specific computational biomechanics may enable accurate, noninvasive assessment of chronic kidney disease. To develop a finite element method (FEM)-based computational elastography framework for estimating renal biomechanical properties from conventional kidney MRI and to evaluate its ability to differentiate chronic kidney disease (CKD) from healthy controls.
  Methods: Kidney segmentation masks from healthy control and CKD subjects were converted into triangulated surface meshes, and watertight surfaces were tetrahedralized to create volumetric FEM meshes. Kidney tissue was modeled as a nearly incompressible Kelvin-Voigt viscoelastic material. Harmonic wave propagation simulations were performed using FEM, followed by Helmholtz-based elastographic inversion to estimate complex shear modulus distributions. Derived biomechanical parameters were used for CKD classification using machine learning models.
  Results: FEM-derived stiffness estimates showed consistency with stiffness ranges previously reported in human renal MRE studies and also showed measurable differences between healthy and CKD groups. Machine learning models trained on FEM-derived features achieved classification performance up to 86.26% cross-validation accuracy with an AUC of 0.92.
  Conclusion: FEM-derived elastographic parameters showed potential for distinguishing CKD from healthy tissue and may provide an alternative for renal biomechanical assessment without dedicated MRE acquisition.

</details>
